FOREWORD: OUR CULTURE IS OUR SAFETY

As Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people, we assert the right to define ourselves,
represent our own stories and chart our own future, founded on the enduring strength
of our cultures. This is the rights of all peoples.
Family Matters is Australia’s only annual report           Yet, Australian governments continue to remove our
written from an Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander      children at unprecedented rates, marginalise our
perspective that monitors Australian governments’          families and devalue our ways of keeping our babies
removal of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander           and children safe. In doing so, governments compel
children from their families. It reflects our unwavering   our children to walk in a singular world imposed upon
commitment to self-determination and accountability        us by colonisation.
for our children’s lives and futures. This includes our
                                                           Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander concepts of
collective right to define success, measure impact,
                                                           safety rest on identity, connection, relationship and
and share the stories of our children, families and
                                                           mutuality (Dudgeon et al, 2014; Rigney et al, 2022;
communities grounded in our ways of knowing,
                                                           AHRC, 2024). For our children, culture is fundamental
being and doing.
                                                           to development; cultural continuity is optimal for
This year marks the tenth edition of Family Matters;       supporting children to thrive. Yet, while Aboriginal
a significant milestone in our ongoing advocacy for        and Torres Strait Islander children have been at
systems and a society that uphold the rights of our        the forefront of policy debates, governments have
children. Over the past decade, this report has borne      repeatedly failed to engage with this evidence in their
witness to the continued failures of child safety          system and practice reforms. They fail to recognise that
systems to keep our children safe and promote              connection and cultural continuity, rather than control
their lifelong wellbeing.                                  or ‘risk management’, is the pathway to safety for
                                                           children, families and communities.

communities for accountability and contributed to          In 2023-24 across Australia, Aboriginal and Torres

There is a growing and unacceptable gap between
the rhetoric of reform and the reality on the ground
for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander children:
we need action, accountability and genuine
partnerships with our communities.
Our culture is the pathway to our children’s safety
and enduring justice for our community.




Our children deserve to be
seen for all that they are:
strong, loved and full of
potential.




Catherine Liddle        Dr. Paul Gray

Co-Chairs
National Family Matters Leadership Group




                                                       THE FAMILY MATTERS REPORT 2025 3
INTRODUCTION

The Family Matters Report (Family Matters) is an annual publication that tracks progress towards
ensuring Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander children and young people grow up safe, cared for,
and connected to their families, communities and culture. A key goal is to eliminate the over-
representation of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander children in out-of-home care (OOHC)
by 2040.
Family Matters is led by SNAICC – National Voice for our
                                                                         1. All families enjoy access to quality,

                                                                                   THE FAMILY MATTERS REPORT 2025 8
 BUILDING BLOCK 4

 Governments and services are accountable to
 Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people.

 12. Establish and resource peak bodies that support
     and enable greater participation of Aboriginal
     and Torres Strait Islander people in shared
     decision making and partnership for policy and
     service design, and in the oversight of systems
     impacting children, in accordance with the
     National Agreement on Closing the Gap Priority
     Reform 1.
 13. Establish Commissioners for Aboriginal and
     Torres Strait Islander Children nationally and in
     every state and territory, in accordance with the
     UN Principles relating to the Status of National
     Institutions, empowered by legislation and
     adequately resourced.
 14. Establish partnerships between Aboriginal
     and Torres Strait Islander communities and
     governments to guide the design, development,
     interpretation and use of data relevant to
     Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander children.
     As a priority, we call on all jurisdictions to address
     data gaps identified throughout this report.
 15. Change the definition and counting rules for
     out-of-home care to include children on
     permanent care orders.


Further details on the recommendations are included
in the conclusion of the Report.

1.1 Responses to child safety                                 Given that these children have been removed from
                                                              their families by child protection authorities, SNAICC
    concerns                                                  and the National Family Matters Leadership Group
                                                              believe that governments must be accountable for the
                                                              separation of these children from their families and,
                                                              as such, they must be included in counts of the data on
OVER-REPRESENTATION IN CHILD                                  children in OOHC. The use of TPPROs and other orders
                                                              is discussed in further detail in Part 1.2 of this report.

Nationally, there has generally been a decrease in the                   are rooted in the failure of successive governments

                                                                                                  THE FAMILY MATTERS REPORT 2025 19
The continuing over-representation also reflects deeply
embedded patterns of systemic bias that are built into
the structures, policies and practices of child protection
systems. These biases compound across different stages
of intervention, and include but are not limited to:
• oversurveillance of and intervention in the lives of
  Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander families,
• racially biased reporting of children and families,
• biased decision-making tools perpetuating inequality,
• discriminatory judgments made by child protection
  decision-making authorities,
• child protection legislation and policy that is heavily
  reliant on non-Indigenous concepts, including those
  of family, wellbeing and best interests of the child,
• the misunderstanding and undervaluing of Aboriginal
  and Torres Strait Islander parenting practices, and
• failure to implement the Aboriginal and Torres Strait
  Islander Child Placement Principle (SNAICC 2024a;
  Haslam et.al. 2023; HREOC 1997; Jenkins and Tilbury
  2024; Malin et al. 1996, AIHW 2025d, etc.).

These structural factors and drivers of entrenchment in
child protection are discussed in more detail in Part 1.3.

PRINCIPLE                                                  STRAIT ISLANDER CHILDREN
The Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Child            Misidentification of Aboriginal and Torres Strait
Placement Principle (the Child Placement Principle)        Islander children occurs at all stages of child
was established in 1984 by Aboriginal and Torres           protection involvement. Inconsistent practices for the
Strait Islander people to address the growing rate of      timely and accurate identification of Aboriginal and
Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander children being       Torres Strait Islander children and families continue
placed in OOHC with, or adopted by, non-Indigenous         to act as a barrier to upholding the rights of Aboriginal
people (SNAICC 2018).                                      and Torres Strait Islander children and young people
The Child Placement Principle recognises the vital role    in contact with child protection systems. This includes
of family, community, culture and Country in ensuring      their right to have their culture recognised, preserved,
the safety and wellbeing of Aboriginal and Torres Strait   and protected (UNCRC Article 30), and the right
Islander children. It affirms that Aboriginal and Torres   of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples to
Strait Islander communities are best placed to make        determine their social and cultural development
decisions about their children.                            (UNDRIP Article 3), including to safeguard the rights
                                                           of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander children.
The Child Placement Principle aims to:
                                                           This misidentification risks depriving children of
• embed culture as central to the safety and wellbeing     culturally safe support, appropriate case planning
  of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander children,       and placements that reflect their identity and
• uphold the rights of Aboriginal and Torres Strait        connection to community.
  Islander children, families and communities in           When children are misidentified, data fails to
  contact with child welfare systems,                      accurately track their interactions with the system.
• strengthen self-determination in child protection        To ensure children receive appropriate support, their
  decision-making, and                                     Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander identity needs
• reduce the over-representation of Aboriginal and         to be established as early as possible, and the basis
  Torres Strait Islander children in child protection      for this determination needs to be recorded. We urge
  and OOHC.                                                child protection authorities to consider this a positive
                                                           obligation to properly explore and document the
The implementation of the Child Placement Principle        cultural identity of all children in a timely and
across jurisdictions has been described as the single      ongoing way.
most significant change affecting welfare practice since   Many Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander parents
the 1970s (HREOC, 1997, p. 379).                           and children often feel uncomfortable disclosing
The Child Placement Principle is structured around         their cultural identity due to well-founded fears of
five core elements: Prevention, Partnership,               adverse treatment within the child protection system.
Participation, Placement and Connection, which             Culturally safe approaches to discussing cultural
together guide efforts to protect the rights of            identity with all children and families are therefore
Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander children and         essential. These approaches help ensure Aboriginal
to maintain their connection to family, community          and Torres Strait Islander children are accurately
and culture.                                               identified and that the rights of every child in contact
Although the Child Placement Principle is                  with the child protection system are respected and
embedded in legislation and policy across Australian       upheld. Safeguards must also be in place to prevent
jurisdictions, implementation remains poor across          the de-identification of Aboriginal and Torres Strait
the country. Implementation Reviews led by SNAICC          Islander children without meaningful consultation
hold governments accountable for their efforts to          with their families, communities and representative
implement the Child Placement Principle in full and        organisations.
continue to identify significant shortfalls in practice.
These reviews find that jurisdictional adherence to the
full intent of the Child Placement Principle, including
making active efforts in prevention, partnership,
participation and connection, is lacking.

Combined, funding for IFSSs and FSSs represented
15.6% of total expenditure on the child protection and
OOHC system in 2023-24, 0.2% less than five years ago.
Table 6 shows a concerning decrease in the proportion
of IFSS expenditure in NSW, Qld and Tas, and a limited
increase across other jurisdictions. Five jurisdictions
reported IFSSs and FSSs expenditure at less than 18%
of total funding, with WA allocating an extremely low
4.4%, far lower than any other jurisdiction.
These figures demonstrate a critical lack of resourcing
for essential primary and secondary services that
prevent progression to tertiary interventions, including
removal to OOHC. Jurisdictions are getting exactly
what they pay for: fewer families supported and more
children in OOHC.

                                                             to ACCOs was reported in Victoria (8%), followed by

ORGANISATIONS                                                prevention-focused service delivery, it is notable that the

                                                             lower at 36%. WA, Tas and the NT did not provide data.
A sustained and meaningful level of funding is required
to enable ACCOs to deliver culturally safe and effective
services for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander
children and families. The extent of investment in
ACCOs serves as a key proxy indicator of the availability
and quality of culturally safe services for Aboriginal and
Torres Strait Islander children and families. Investment
should be aligned with the relative level of need at each
stage of the child protection system, with an emphasis
on early support and prevention. While this indicator is
intended to reflect investment directed to Aboriginal and
Torres Strait Islander community-based approaches,
it is unclear if there is consistency across jurisdictions
regarding interpretations of what constitutes an ACCO,
as distinct from other Aboriginal and Torres Strait
Islander organisations and services.

1.2 Out-of-home care

 CARE AND PROTECTION ORDERS                                 GUARDIANSHIP AND CUSTODY
                                                            ORDERS
 There are several types of care and protection orders
 available to statutory child protection agencies.          Most children in OOHC are on a finalised guardianship
 The names of these orders differ across states             or custody order (GCO).
 and territories, but are reported on in consolidated
 categories by the AIHW (AIHW 2025, Table S4.10).           Guardianship orders involve the transfer of legal
 Third-party parental responsibility orders (TPPROs)        responsibility for a child’s long-term welfare to
 and guardianship and custody orders (GCOs) (both           a state or territory agency or a non-government
 described below) account for the vast majority of          agency. These orders represent a significant level
 children on orders. Finalised supervisory orders,          of intervention in both the child’s life and their
 interim and temporary orders and administrative            family circumstances and are intended for use
 arrangements account for a much smaller number             only when other options have been exhausted.
 of children (AIHW 2025, Table S4.10).                      Under a guardianship order, the guardian assumes
                                                            responsibility for major decisions affecting the
                                                            child’s wellbeing, such as education, health care,
                                                            living arrangements and financial matters. However,
                                                            guardianship does not include authority over the
 THIRD-PARTY PARENTAL                                       child’s daily care and control, which is instead
 RESPONSIBILITY ORDERS                                      conferred through custody orders. They can be
                                                            short or long-term orders (AIHW 2025f).
 Third-party parental responsibility orders (TPPROs)
 may be ordered if a parent is assessed as being            Custody orders place a child in the care of the
 unable to care for a child or children. Under the order,   relevant statutory child protection or a non-
 parental responsibility is transferred to a relative       government agency, granting responsibility for
 or other nominated person(s), such as an officer of        the child’s day-to-day needs and supervision.
 the state or territory department, whom the court          In these arrangements, the parent often retains
 considers appropriate. The order transfers all duties,     legal guardianship, meaning they continue to hold
 powers, responsibilities and authority to which            responsibility for long-term decisions. Custody alone
 parents are entitled by law. They can be short             does not extend to matters concerning the child’s
 or long-term orders (AIHW 2025f).                          broader welfare or future planning. They can be
                                                            short or long-term orders (AIHW 2025f).*
 TPPROs are often excluded from OOHC statistics
                                                            * Short and long-term care arrangements
 presented by state, territory and federal governments        Short and long-term care arrangements refer to arrangements made
 because, once finalised, the government department           under legal orders which are put in place for a specified period. For
 no longer holds legal responsibility for the child,          short-term orders, this would be for a specified period of 2 years or
                                                              less. Long-term orders, greater than 2 years, generally until the child
 even though the child remains in long-term care.             turns 18 (AIHW 2025f).
 Excluding children on TPPROs from the count of
                                                              Finalised orders

REMOVAL INTO OOHC                                           • suffer abuse while in OOHC,

Removing a child from their family to place them in           and become ‘crossover kids’ (Yoorrook Justice
OOHC is a deeply traumatic experience, not only for           Commission 2023 and 2025; AHRC 1997; AIHW 2025;
the child or children but for each and every member           CCYP 2021; AIFS 2023; Lima et al., 2020; McDowall
of their family and extended kin known to the child.          et al., 2025; SNAICC 2018).
The overwhelming majority of children will retain vivid
memories of the moment and continue to relive it
throughout their lives. For those who do not, they will
carry the story of their removal from their birth parents
for the rest of their lives. Removal signifies a severing   ADMITTANCE TO AND DISCHARGE
of bonds and, in most cases, does not reflect the child     FROM OOHC
or their parents’ wishes.

Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander children are
more likely to:
• be placed in a residential care service, the least
  preferred OOHC option under the Child Placement
  Principle hierarchy of placements,
• be removed to an environment that lacks cultural
  safety or understanding (for example, foster care
  with non-Indigenous carers),
• spend longer in OOHC,
• have more placements,
• have lower reunification rates,
• be reported missing from placement,
• leave school before completing secondary education,
• become parents while in OOHC or soon after leaving,

Torres Strait Islander peoples in which parental rights      (Torres Strait Islander Traditional Child Rearing Practice)
and responsibilities are transferred, by customary           Act 2020 (the Act) formally recognised Ailan Kastom
agreement, from a child’s birth parents to trusted           within the legal system. This legislation affirms the
relatives or community members. Practiced since time         right of Torres Strait Islander communities to define and
immemorial across Zenadth Kes (the Torres Strait             uphold their own systems of care and uphold cultural
Islands), Ailan Kastom reflects deep cultural values of      identity, and the Act shows that legislation can create
kinship, collective care and continuity of identity.         space for Indigenous legal frameworks through which

culture in his life.

and culture.

THE FAMILY MATTERS REPORT 2025 71
1.3 Social determinants of wellbeing

 COLONIAL LEGACIES AND THE PATH TO INTERVENTION
 When the statutory child protection system intervenes in our families to remove our children, it is typically
 framed as a matter of determining what is in the child’s ‘best interest’. Within this framework, structural
 determinants like poverty, housing insecurity, intergenerational trauma and systemic racism are routinely
 recast not as consequences of colonisation but, rather, as indicators of parental failure.
 The structural drivers of the over-representation of our children in care are not recent developments. They are
 the legacy of Australia’s harmful colonial past, which continues to the present. Aboriginal and Torres Strait
 Islander families are disproportionately targeted, not because they pose greater risk, but because they live
 with greater systemic disadvantage and discrimination. Parents’ feelings of powerlessness after children are
 removed and the challenges of maintaining relationships with their removed children have been found to have
 strong parallels with the experiences of Aboriginal mothers during the Stolen Generations (Payne 2021).
 While policy frameworks make repeated claims to promote self-determination and culturally responsive
 approaches, the criteria for child protection intervention continue to marginalise community approaches and
 punish social disadvantage rather than respond to it. The over-representation of Aboriginal and Torres Strait
 Islander children in OOHC is a predictable outcome of a system that continues to evaluate Aboriginal and
 Torres Strait Islander parenting through a Western lens and define it as inadequate, rather than understanding
 differences and recognising strengths (Wright et al. 2025).
 Western constructs of wellbeing and nuclear family inform how child protection services define a ‘safe’
 home. Behind the bureaucratic language of risk assessments, safety plans, and child-centred practice lies
 a system rooted in Western assumptions about health, wellbeing and family. Consequently, the lived realities
 of many Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander families, including extended kinship care, shared responsibility
 for children and cultural practices, are not only often overlooked but are actively misinterpreted (Malin et al.
 1996). In a system built to reflect and reinforce the norms of a dominant culture, cultural difference is viewed
 not as diversity but as a deficiency.
 Reversing over-representation not only requires governments to take action to address its structural drivers;
 governments must also reform the statutory child protection system to acknowledge the inherent strength of
 Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander communities to raise our own children.




                                                                                     THE FAMILY MATTERS REPORT 2025 72

Disadvantage becomes criminalised when Aboriginal
and Torres Strait Islander children do not have access
to appropriate supports and services, and their
communities are overpoliced. For instance, laws that
regulate the use of public space or punish ‘crimes
of necessity’ criminalise Aboriginal and Torres Strait
Islander children for minor offences like fare evading
on public transport or stealing food (ALRC 2018; Walsh
2019).

CASE STUDY
GUMBAYNGGIRR GIINGANA FREEDOM SCHOOL

CULTURE IS THE CORE ON                                   THE GGFS MODEL & IMPACT
GUMBAYNGGIRR COUNTRY
                                                         GGFS is founded on the principle that children have
Gumbaynggirr Giingana Freedom School (GGFS) is           the right to learn through language and culture while
the first bilingual Aboriginal language school in NSW,   achieving full academic outcomes. Under the leadership
placing culture at the core of its model, pedagogy and   of Gumbaynggirr and Bundjalung man Clark Webb
vision. GGFS opened in 2022 on Gumbaynggirr Country      (CEO), Gumbaynggirr woman Ellie Buchanan (Language
in Garlambirla/Coffs Harbour and is an independent,      Director) and Bundjalung man Glen Cook (Principal),
Aboriginal-led school run by the Bularri Muurlay         the school delivers education that is culturally grounded
Nyanggan Aboriginal Corporation (BMNAC).                 and academically strong. Its pedagogy is guided by
                                                         three pillars: Wajaarr (Country), Guunu-warluuny
                                                         (Culture) and Miindalay-gam (Wisdom). Students
                                                         engage daily in song, dance, storytelling and on-Country
                                                         learning while meeting NSW Education Standards
                                                         Authority requirements in curriculum, assessment,
                                                         teaching quality and school standards.
                                                         Classrooms initially paired teachers with language
                                                         specialists, but due to the growth of all staff and
                                                         students, the school is now transitioning to immersion.
                                                         The school uses the Accelerated Second Language
                                                         Acquisition (ASLA) method for language teaching,
                                                         which was developed by Arapaho linguist Dr Neyooxet
                                                         Greymorning and has been used globally to revive
                                                         First Nations languages for more than 20 years. ASLA
                                                         mirrors the way children acquire their first language,
                                                         prioritising oral comprehension, repetition, gestures
                                                         and context-rich vocabulary.

Strength in How We Raise Our Own

There is an intrinsic strength in how Aboriginal   Our kinship systems defy the nuclear family
and Torres Strait Islander communities raise       model imposed by colonisation. Aunties,
our children, grounded in millennia-old            uncles, grandparents and cousins all hold
kinship systems and collective responsibility.     vital and unique roles in nurturing and guiding
These systems are not a simple alternative         our young ones. This collective way to raise
to Eurocentric perspectives of child-rearing.      children acts as a web of safety.
They are holistic, deeply relational and rooted
in cultural continuity. They are established       When our young ones are held within culture,
through the Dreaming and grounded in               they thrive. They know who they are,
connection to Country, to lore, and to one         where they come from, and that they
another.                                           are held by many.

Every act of raising a child within our            Across communities, we see strength
communities is an act of cultural transmission.    through our ability to reclaim cultural child-
It passes down not just knowledge, but identity,   raising practices, weaving together ancestral
belonging and resilience. This is how we           knowledge with new strategies to protect and
nurture our children: through culture,             uplift our children in today’s world. Our people
through connection, and through care.              continue to innovate from a place of strength.
                                                   Whether through community-led childhood
                                                   programs, local decision-making models or
                                                   grassroots movements.

There has been some positive progress:
• In June 2025, TAC and VACCA co-delivered culturally
  responsive and trauma informed training through
  the SNAICC Waterways Project, with 27 service
  providers participating.
• Child Safety recently lifted staff delegations to
  strengthen cultural safety in decision-making for
  Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander children.
  However, these adjustments were reactive rather
  than co-designed. The challenge now is to shift
  from short-term fixes to proactive reforms that are
  embedded in Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander
  authority.

Children and young people, ACCOs and non-government
service providers continue to raise concerns with

increase in funding to support children and young
people to access the DFFH initiative, Home Stretch.
Publicly available data13 shows that, in 2023-2024, there
were 702 Intensive Family Support Cases (up from 432 in
the previous year) in regional and remote locations, with
only 163 referrals to Intensive Family Support Services,
two fewer referrals than the previous year. In 2023-24,
the total number of cases was 1703, compared to 1659
in 2022-23, and the total referrals decreased from 510 in
2022-2023 to 449 in 2023-2024. This is highly concerning
as investment in intensive family support and earlier
intervention are crucial components of reducing the
number of children in OOHC.

meet the requirements under Building Block 2. It is
acknowledged that commissioning for ACCO services

where we are heading.                                        BUILDING BLOCK 1

truth-telling, for amplifying Aboriginal and Torres Strait
Islander voices, and for centring what matters most:          BUILDING BLOCK 1
